# CONDITIONS SUBSTRATE MODEL  
### (Canon‑Aligned Structural Module)

## 🧩 1. Core Identity of the Module  
**Purpose:**  
To formally map how *conditions* — environmental, informational, emotional, structural, or systemic — alter human or system behavior.  
Conditions are not “inputs” or “stimuli.” They are **regime‑shaping fields** that modify the *baseline operating state* of an agent or system.

**Why this matters:**  
PsyOps, governance, media, and structural manipulation all rely on the same principle:

> **If you change the conditions, you change the behavior — without ever touching the agent.**

This module provides the canonical grammar for detecting, classifying, and manipulating conditions safely.

---

# 🧬 2. Canon Definition  
A **Condition** is a *persistent, ambient modifier* that shifts the **Regime State** of an agent or system.

Formally:

\[
C : S \rightarrow S'
\]

Where:

- \( S \) = baseline regime  
- \( S' \) = altered regime  
- \( C \) = condition field  
- The agent’s internal logic remains unchanged; only the *operational envelope* shifts.

This is why conditions are so powerful — they bypass cognition entirely.

---

# 🧭 3. Condition Classes (Triadic Canon)

## **1. Structural Conditions**  
These modify the *environmental constraints* around an agent.

Examples:
- scarcity vs abundance  
- time pressure  
- surveillance presence  
- access pathways  
- mobility constraints  
- resource distribution  

**Reference:** Structural Detection Module  
→ Conditions are the *ambient layer* that structural detection reveals.

---

## **2. Emotional Conditions**  
These modify the *affective baseline* of an agent.

Examples:
- fear  
- safety  
- belonging  
- isolation  
- uncertainty  
- anticipation  

**Reference:** Emotions Module  
→ Emotional conditions are the *field-level* version of emotional states.

---

## **3. Informational Conditions**  
These modify the *information substrate* surrounding an agent.

Examples:
- noise  
- clarity  
- overload  
- deprivation  
- narrative dominance  
- framing  

**Reference:** Media Substrate Model  
→ Informational conditions are the *ambient media field* that shapes perception.

---

## **4. Governance Conditions**  
These modify the *rules, incentives, and enforcement landscape*.

Examples:
- stability  
- volatility  
- legitimacy  
- opacity  
- accountability  
- coercion  

**Reference:** Governance Substrate Model  
→ Governance conditions define the *regime envelope* in which agents operate.

---

# 🧩 4. Condition Hooks & Stacks  
Conditions rarely act alone. They form **stacks** — layered fields that combine into a composite regime.

### **Condition Hook**  
A hook is a *specific attachment point* where a condition can influence behavior.

Examples:
- cognitive load  
- emotional vulnerability  
- structural bottleneck  
- narrative anchor  
- identity resonance  

### **Condition Stack**  
A stack is a *multi-layered condition field* that produces emergent behavior.

Example stack:
- scarcity (structural)  
- urgency (emotional)  
- narrative compression (informational)  
- enforcement opacity (governance)  

This stack reliably produces:
- compliance  
- tunnel vision  
- reduced creativity  
- increased susceptibility to external influence  

This is why PsyOps uses stacks, not single conditions.

---

# 🧠 5. Condition Regime Shifts  
Conditions produce **regime transitions**, not “behavior changes.”

Three canonical transitions:

### **1. Narrowing Regime**  
Reduced options, increased predictability.  
Used in coercive governance, crisis media, and high-pressure environments.

### **2. Expanding Regime**  
Increased options, increased creativity.  
Used in innovation, education, and empowerment systems.

### **3. Oscillating Regime**  
Rapid alternation between narrowing and expanding.  
Used in manipulation, addiction cycles, and destabilization operations.

---

# 🔍 6. Detection Protocol  
Conditions are detected through **ambient field analysis**, not direct observation.

Detection layers:

1. **Structural indicators**  
2. **Emotional field resonance**  
3. **Media substrate patterns**  
4. **Governance envelope shifts**  
5. **Behavioral drift signatures**

This is where your **Structural Detection Module** plugs in.

---

# 🧱 7. Canon Integration Map  
This module references and integrates:

- **Structural Detection** → reveals structural conditions  
- **Emotions** → maps emotional conditions  
- **Media Substrate Model** → maps informational conditions  
- **Governance Substrate Model** → maps governance conditions  
- **Hooks & Stacks** → mechanism layer  
- **Regime Maps** → condition-induced transitions  
- **Opacity / Mode / TEL** → field-level modifiers  
- **Incident Substrate Model** → acute condition spikes  
- **Resilience Checker** → condition resistance profiles  

This module is a **hub** — it connects almost every other substrate.

# ⭐ Core Insight  
A **Condition** is not an event, not a stimulus, not a state, not a label.  
A condition is:

> **A field that modifies the operating regime of a system.**

This is the key that lets us unify *all* conditions — physical, emotional, informational, structural, social, economic, temporal, computational, biological — under one substrate.

To do that, we need a **single mathematical backbone** that scales across dimensions.

---

# 🧩 1. The Universal Structure of a Condition  
Every condition, no matter the domain, has the same four components:

### **1. Carrier**  
The medium through which the condition exists.  
Examples: environment, information, emotion, governance, physics, resource distribution.

### **2. Modifier**  
The specific alteration applied to the system.  
Examples: scarcity, abundance, pressure, noise, clarity, volatility, isolation.

### **3. Regime Target**  
Which part of the system is affected.  
Examples: attention, mobility, decision bandwidth, emotional baseline, structural pathways.

### **4. Regime Effect**  
How the system’s behavior envelope changes.  
Examples: narrowing, expanding, oscillating, stabilizing, destabilizing.

This gives us a universal grammar:

\[
Condition = (Carrier, Modifier, Target, Effect)
\]

This is domain‑agnostic and scales with your dimensional model.

---

# 🧮 2. The Equation You’re Looking For  
You want a single equation that works for *any* condition and plugs into RTT dimensional math.

Here it is:

\[
C(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

Where:

- \( d \) = dimension (structural, emotional, informational, temporal, etc.)
- \( M(d) \) = modifier intensity in that dimension  
- \( F(d) \) = field coupling coefficient (how strongly the system responds in that dimension)

The condition’s total effect across all dimensions:

\[
C_{total} = \sum_{d=1}^{n} M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

This is the **scalar** you were asking for — the unified condition magnitude.

It’s compatible with:
- regime transitions  
- rhythmic lifting  
- coherence/clarity equations  
- drift maps  
- structural detection  
- media substrate  
- governance substrate  

Everything plugs in.

---

# 🧭 3. The 3W’s (your “recommended usage” layer)  
You mentioned wanting a simple, coherent model for practical use.  
Here’s the canonical 3W layer:

### **What is the condition?**  
Carrier + Modifier  
(“Environmental scarcity”, “Informational overload”, “Emotional isolation”)

### **Where does it act?**  
Regime Target  
(“Decision bandwidth”, “Mobility”, “Narrative susceptibility”)

### **What does it do?**  
Regime Effect  
(“Narrowing”, “Expanding”, “Oscillating”, “Stabilizing”)

This gives analysts, designers, or auditors a clean interface.

---

Not a list of conditions — a *matrix* that can generate any condition.

### **B. Regime Targets (from your dimensional model)**  
Each dimension becomes a targetable regime.

### **C. Regime Effects (canonical 4‑state model)**  
Narrow, expand, oscillate, stabilize.

### **D. Condition Dynamics (equations above)**  
This is the engine.

This makes the module future‑proof and domain‑agnostic.

---

# 🧠 5. Why This Works for “Everything Is Fair Game”  
Because the substrate doesn’t care *what* the condition is — only how it modifies the regime.

Examples:

- Weather → Carrier: physical; Modifier: volatility  
- Social isolation → Carrier: emotional; Modifier: deprivation  
- Algorithmic ranking → Carrier: informational; Modifier: compression  
- Economic inflation → Carrier: structural; Modifier: scarcity  
- Time pressure → Carrier: temporal; Modifier: compression  
- Governance opacity → Carrier: governance; Modifier: uncertainty  

All of these reduce to the same math.

# ⭐ CONDITIONS × DIMENSIONS  
### The coupling model that makes conditions computationally meaningful

Every condition has a **modifier intensity** \( M(d) \) in each dimension, and every system has a **coupling coefficient** \( F(d) \) in each dimension.

The condition’s effect in a dimension:

\[
C(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

The total regime impact:

\[
C_{total} = \sum_{d=1}^{n} M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

This is the universal backbone.

---

# ⭐ DIMENSIONAL MAP  
Below is the **canonical RTT dimensional map** for conditions.  
Each dimension has:

- **What it represents**  
- **How conditions couple to it**  
- **Typical carriers/modifiers that activate it**  
- **Example targets**  
- **Effect tendencies** (narrow/expand/oscillate/stabilize)

---

## **D0 — Structural Dimension**  
**Represents:** physical layout, access, pathways, constraints.  
**Coupling:** high for structural carriers; moderate for governance.  
**Activated by:** scarcity, bottlenecks, abundance, volatility.  
**Targets:** mobility, resource_access, structural_pathways.  
**Effects:** narrowing, stabilizing.

\[

### **Option D — Condition Stacks Engine**  
Nonlinear interactions, critical points, composite regime fields.

---

You’re at the fun part now—the place where conditions stop being isolated levers and start behaving like weather systems.

Here’s a clean, canon‑aligned **Condition Stacks Engine** you can drop into `c_Capture.md` as the “mechanism” section.

---

### 1. What is a condition stack?

A **Condition Stack** is an ordered set of conditions that act together as a single composite regime field.

\[
Stack = \{ C_1, C_2, ..., C_k \}
\]

Each \( C_i \) is a condition:

\[
C_i = (Carrier_i, Modifier_i, Target_i, Effect_i)
\]

And each has dimensional impact:

\[
C_i(d) = M_i(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

---

### 2. Composite regime field

The stack’s **composite field** in dimension \( d \):

\[
C_{stack}(d) = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i(d) \cdot C_i(d) + I(d)
\]

- **\( w_i(d) \):** weight of condition \( i \) in dimension \( d \) (priority, dominance, recency, etc.)  
- **\( I(d) \):** interaction term (nonlinear effects between conditions)

Total stack impact:

\[
C_{stack,total} = \sum_{d=1}^{n} C_{stack}(d)
\]

This is the scalar you use for regime thresholds, rhythmic lifting, and risk.

---

### 3. Interaction rules (nonlinear engine)

You don’t want stacks to be just sums—here’s the minimal nonlinear rule set:

**1. Reinforcing interactions**

- Same Effect (e.g., multiple narrowing conditions)  
- Same Target or strongly coupled Targets  
- Result: \( I(d) > 0 \) → super‑linear impact

**2. Counteracting interactions**

- Opposing Effects (narrowing vs expanding, stabilizing vs destabilizing)  
- Result: \( I(d) < 0 \) → partial cancellation, buffering

**3. Oscillation induction**

- Alternating Effects (narrowing + expanding with temporal or informational carriers)  
- Result: regime enters **oscillating** state even if \( C_{stack,total} \) is moderate

**4. Critical points**

- When \( C_{stack}(d) \) crosses a threshold, regime flips state:
  - baseline → narrowed  
  - baseline → destabilized  
  - narrowed → destabilized  
  - oscillating → failure

---

### 4. Stack representation for capture

You can express stacks in capture like this:

```text
Stack:
  - C1: (Carrier=structural, Modifier=scarcity, Target=resource_access, Effect=narrowing)
  - C2: (Carrier=informational, Modifier=noise, Target=attention, Effect=narrowing)
  - C3: (Carrier=governance, Modifier=opacity, Target=narrative_susceptibility, Effect=oscillating)

Computed:
  C_stack(d) = Σ w_i(d) * C_i(d) + I(d)
  Regime: narrowed + oscillating (high risk)
```

---

### 5. Minimal canonical rules for the module

You can add this as a short rules block:

- **Rule 1:** Stacks are ordered sets; order can encode time or priority.  
- **Rule 2:** Each stack must compute \( C_{stack}(d) \) and \( C_{stack,total} \).  
- **Rule 3:** Interaction term \( I(d) \) is required when:
  - multiple conditions share Target, or  
  - Effects conflict, or  
  - temporal carriers are present.  
- **Rule 4:** Regime state is determined from \( C_{stack,total} \) and dominant Effects.  
- **Rule 5:** High \( C_{stack,total} \) or oscillating regimes must be flagged for audit.

---

# `c_Capture.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Canonical Capture**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Substrate / Regime‑Modifier  
**Purpose:** Provide a unified, dimensional grammar for capturing any condition—physical, structural, informational, emotional, temporal, social, economic, computational, or governance—as a *regime‑modifying field*.

Conditions do not describe events or states.  
Conditions describe **fields that alter the operating regime of a system**.

---

## **2. What a Condition *Is***  
A **Condition** is a 4‑tuple:

\[
Condition = (Carrier, Modifier, Target, Effect)
\]

Where:

- **Carrier** — the medium through which the condition exists  
- **Modifier** — the alteration applied to the carrier  
- **Target** — the regime component affected  
- **Effect** — the resulting regime shift  

This tuple is universal across all domains.

---

## **3. Condition Grammar (Core Matrix)**

### **3.1 Carrier**  
Medium of the condition.

```
physical
structural
informational
emotional
temporal
social
economic
computational
governance
other
```

### **3.2 Modifier**  
Alteration applied to the carrier.

```
scarcity
abundance
pressure
volatility
noise
clarity
overload
deprivation
compression
expansion
opacity
transparency
stability
instability
custom
```

### **3.3 Target**  
Regime component affected.

```
attention
mobility
decision_bandwidth
emotional_baseline
structural_pathways
narrative_susceptibility
resource_access
time_window
identity_resonance
system_specific
```

### **3.4 Effect**  
Regime shift produced.

```
narrowing
expanding
oscillating
stabilizing
destabilizing
neutral
```

---

## **4. Dimensional Model Integration**

Conditions operate across RTT dimensions.  
Each modifier has intensity \( M(d) \).  
Each system has coupling coefficient \( F(d) \).

### **4.1 Per‑Dimension Equation**

\[
C(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

### **4.2 Aggregate Condition Impact**

\[
C_{total} = \sum_{d=1}^{n} M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

### **4.3 Dimensions**

```
D0 Structural
D1 Informational
D2 Emotional
D3 Temporal
D4 Social
D5 Economic
D6 Computational
D7 Governance
```

Each dimension has:

- **sensitivity** (F(d))  
- **modifier intensity** (M(d))  
- **regime thresholds**  

---

## **5. Capture Protocol (Operator‑Grade)**

### **Step 1 — Identify Carrier**
What medium is the condition expressed through?

### **Step 2 — Identify Modifier**
What alteration is present in that medium?

### **Step 3 — Identify Target**
Which regime component is affected?

### **Step 4 — Identify Effect**
What regime shift results?

### **Step 5 — Dimensionalize**
Assign \( M(d) \) across all relevant dimensions.

### **Step 6 — Compute**
Calculate:

\[
C(d),\; C_{total}
\]

### **Step 7 — Interpret**
Determine regime state:

- baseline  
- narrowed  
- expanded  
- oscillating  
- stabilized  
- destabilized  

---

## **6. Condition Stacks Engine**

A **Condition Stack** is an ordered set of conditions:

\[
Stack = \{ C_1, C_2, ..., C_k \}
\]

### **6.1 Composite Field**

\[
C_{stack}(d) = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i(d) \cdot C_i(d) + I(d)
\]

Where:

- \( w_i(d) \) = weight of condition i  
- \( I(d) \) = interaction term  

### **6.2 Interaction Rules**

- **Reinforcing** → super‑linear  
- **Counteracting** → partial cancellation  
- **Oscillation induction** → alternating effects  
- **Critical points** → regime flips  

### **6.3 Stack Regime**

\[
C_{stack,total} = \sum_{d} C_{stack}(d)
\]

Regime determined by:

- dominant Effects  
- dimensional spikes  
- critical thresholds  

---

## **7. Capture Examples**

### **Example 1 — Informational Noise**
```
Carrier: informational
Modifier: noise
Target: attention
Effect: narrowing
```

### **Example 2 — Temporal Compression**
```
Carrier: temporal
Modifier: compression
Target: decision_bandwidth
Effect: narrowing
```

### **Example 3 — Governance Opacity**
```
Carrier: governance
Modifier: opacity
Target: narrative_susceptibility
Effect: oscillating
```

### **Example Stack**
```
C1: informational noise → narrowing
C2: temporal compression → narrowing
C3: governance opacity → oscillating

Composite: narrowed + oscillating (high risk)
```

---

## **8. Integration Map**

This capture integrates with:

- Structural Detection  
- Emotions Substrate  
- Media Substrate  
- Governance Substrate  
- Hooks & Stacks  
- Regime Maps  
- Opacity / Mode / TEL  
- Incident Substrate  
- Resilience Checker  

Conditions are a **hub substrate**.

---

## **9. Notes for Operators**

- Conditions modify **regimes**, not identities.  
- Conditions are **ambient**, not event‑based.  
- Conditions scale across **all RTT dimensions**.  
- Stacks produce **nonlinear composite fields**.  
- High \( C_{total} \) requires **audit**.  

---

# `c_Stacks.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Condition Stacks Engine**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Composite Field Engine  
**Purpose:** Define how multiple conditions combine into a single composite regime field, including weighting, dimensional aggregation, nonlinear interactions, and regime transitions.

A **Condition Stack** is the mechanism that transforms isolated conditions into a unified field capable of producing complex regime shifts.

---

## **2. Definition of a Condition Stack**

A **Condition Stack** is an ordered set of conditions:

\[
Stack = \{ C_1,\; C_2,\; ...,\; C_k \}
\]

Each condition \( C_i \) is a canonical 4‑tuple:

\[
C_i = (Carrier_i,\; Modifier_i,\; Target_i,\; Effect_i)
\]

Each condition has dimensional impact:

\[
C_i(d) = M_i(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

Where:

- \( M_i(d) \) = modifier intensity in dimension \( d \)  
- \( F(d) \) = system coupling coefficient in dimension \( d \)

---

## **3. Composite Regime Field**

The stack produces a **composite field** per dimension:

\[
C_{stack}(d) = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i(d) \cdot C_i(d) + I(d)
\]

Where:

- **\( w_i(d) \)** — weight of condition \( i \) in dimension \( d \)  
  - priority  
  - recency  
  - dominance  
  - carrier relevance  
- **\( I(d) \)** — nonlinear interaction term  
  - reinforcing  
  - counteracting  
  - oscillation induction  
  - critical‑point amplification  

Total stack impact:

\[
C_{stack,total} = \sum_{d=1}^{n} C_{stack}(d)
\]

This scalar determines regime transitions.

---

## **4. Interaction Model (Nonlinear Engine)**

Condition stacks are **not** linear sums.  
They follow canonical interaction rules:

### **4.1 Reinforcing Interactions**
Occurs when:

- multiple conditions share the same **Effect**  
- or share the same **Target**  
- or activate the same **dimension**

\[
I(d) > 0
\]

Produces **super‑linear narrowing**, **super‑linear stabilizing**, etc.

---

### **4.2 Counteracting Interactions**
Occurs when:

- Effects oppose (narrowing vs expanding)  
- or Targets buffer each other  
- or dimensions have inverse coupling

\[
I(d) < 0
\]

Produces **partial cancellation** or **regime buffering**.

---

### **4.3 Oscillation Induction**
Occurs when:

- alternating Effects (narrowing + expanding)  
- temporal carriers  
- informational compression + governance opacity  
- emotional instability + narrative volatility

\[
I(d) \rightarrow oscillatory
\]

Produces **oscillating regimes** even when \( C_{stack,total} \) is moderate.

---

### **4.4 Critical Points**
Occurs when:

\[
C_{stack}(d) > Threshold(d)
\]

Triggers regime flips:

- baseline → narrowed  
- narrowed → destabilized  
- oscillating → failure  
- expanded → destabilized  

Critical points are dimension‑specific.

---

## **5. Stack Capture Format (Operator‑Grade)**

A stack is captured as:

```
Stack:
  - C1: (Carrier, Modifier, Target, Effect)
  - C2: (Carrier, Modifier, Target, Effect)
  - C3: ...
```

Then dimensionalized:

```
C_stack(d) = Σ w_i(d) * C_i(d) + I(d)
C_stack,total = Σ C_stack(d)
Regime = {narrowed | expanded | oscillating | stabilized | destabilized}
```

---

## **6. Canonical Stack Examples**

### **6.1 Crisis Compression Stack**
```
C1: informational noise → narrowing
C2: temporal compression → narrowing
C3: structural scarcity → narrowing
```

Composite:
- Reinforcing across D1, D3, D0  
- Strong positive interaction \( I(d) \)

**Regime:** Hard‑narrowed (critical).

---

### **6.2 Oscillation Induction Stack**
```
C1: governance opacity → oscillating
C2: informational overload → narrowing
C3: emotional isolation → narrowing
```

Composite:
- Opacity + overload → oscillation  
- Isolation amplifies oscillation  

**Regime:** Oscillating + narrowed (high instability).

---

### **6.3 Stabilization Stack**
```
C1: social cohesion → stabilizing
C2: informational clarity → stabilizing
C3: governance legitimacy → stabilizing
```

Composite:
- Reinforcing stabilizing field  
- Negative interaction against destabilizing dimensions

**Regime:** Stabilized.

---

## **7. Dimensional Stack Example**

### **Stack**
```
C1: temporal compression
C2: informational noise
C3: economic volatility
```

### **Dimensional Intensities**
```
M1(D3) = high
M2(D1) = high
M3(D5) = medium
```

### **Coupling**
```
F(D3) = high
F(D1) = high
F(D5) = medium
```

### **Computed**
```
C_stack(D3) = high × high = extreme
C_stack(D1) = high × high = extreme
C_stack(D5) = medium × medium = moderate
C_stack,total = extreme + extreme + moderate
```

**Regime:** Severe narrowing + volatility oscillation.

---

## **8. Operator Rules for Stacks**

- Stacks must compute **per‑dimension fields**.  
- Stacks must compute **interaction terms**.  
- Stacks must compute **C_stack,total**.  
- Stacks must determine **regime state**.  
- High \( C_{stack,total} \) or oscillation requires **audit**.  
- Stacks may be **time‑ordered** or **priority‑ordered**.  
- Stacks may be decomposed into **carrier clusters**.

---

## **9. Integration Map**

Stacks integrate with:

- Dimensional Model  
- Regime Maps  
- Hooks & Stacks Substrate  
- Structural Detection  
- Media Substrate  
- Governance Substrate  
- Incident Substrate  
- Resilience Checker  

Stacks are the **mechanism layer** of the Conditions Substrate Model.

# `c_Analyzer.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Analyzer Suite**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Analyzer Layer  
**Purpose:** Provide computational instruments for evaluating conditions, dimensional fields, stacks, regime transitions, resilience, and propagation.

Analyzers convert captured conditions into **operational intelligence**.

---

# **2. Analyzer Overview**

The Conditions Substrate Model includes four canonical analyzers:

1. **Condition Intensity Analyzer**  
2. **Regime Shift Analyzer**  
3. **Stack Analyzer**  
4. **Resilience Analyzer**

Each analyzer uses the same dimensional backbone:

\[
C(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

\[
C_{total} = \sum_{d=1}^{n} C(d)
\]

---

# **3. Condition Intensity Analyzer**  
### **Purpose**  
Compute the dimensional and aggregate impact of a single condition.

### **Inputs**
```
Condition_Capture
Dimensional_Profile
```

### **Outputs**
```
Condition_Vector (C(d) per dimension)
Condition_Scalar (C_total)
```

### **Method**
1. Extract modifier intensities \( M(d) \).  
2. Retrieve coupling coefficients \( F(d) \).  
3. Compute per‑dimension field:

\[
C(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

4. Compute aggregate field:

\[
C_{total} = \sum_{d} C(d)
\]

### **Interpretation**
- High C(d) → dimensional spike  
- High C_total → regime shift likelihood  
- Dominant dimension → primary regime driver  

---

# **4. Regime Shift Analyzer**  
### **Purpose**  
Determine the regime state produced by a condition or stack.

### **Inputs**
```
Condition_Vector
Condition_Scalar
Regime_State (baseline)
```

### **Outputs**
```
Regime_Shift_Profile
```

### **Regime Thresholds**
Each dimension has thresholds:

```
narrow_threshold(d)
expand_threshold(d)
oscillate_threshold(d)
destabilize_threshold(d)
```

### **Method**
1. Compare C(d) to thresholds.  
2. Identify dominant Effects.  
3. Compute regime transition:

\[
Regime_{new} = f(C(d), C_{total}, Effect)
\]

### **Regime Logic**
- **Narrowing:** high C(d) in D1, D3, D0  
- **Expanding:** high C(d) in D2, D4  
- **Oscillating:** alternating Effects or temporal/informational/governance spikes  
- **Stabilizing:** high C(d) in D4, D7  
- **Destabilizing:** high C(d) in D5, D7 or multi‑dimensional spikes  

### **Output Format**
```
Regime: narrowed
Drivers: D1 informational, D3 temporal
Magnitude: high
Notes: attention compression + time pressure
```

---

# **5. Stack Analyzer**  
### **Purpose**  
Evaluate composite fields produced by multiple conditions.

### **Inputs**
```
Condition_Stack
Dimensional_Profile
```

### **Outputs**
```
Composite_Regime_Field
Risk_Profile
```

### **Composite Field Equation**

\[
C_{stack}(d) = \sum_{i=1}^{k} w_i(d) \cdot C_i(d) + I(d)
\]

Where:

- \( w_i(d) \) = weight of condition i  
- \( I(d) \) = nonlinear interaction term  

### **Interaction Types**
- **Reinforcing** → super‑linear  
- **Counteracting** → partial cancellation  
- **Oscillation induction** → alternating Effects  
- **Critical point amplification** → threshold crossing  

### **Stack Regime**

\[
C_{stack,total} = \sum_{d} C_{stack}(d)
\]

Regime determined by:

- dominant Effects  
- dimensional spikes  
- interaction patterns  
- critical thresholds  

### **Risk Profile**
```
risk_level: {low | medium | high | critical}
risk_drivers: [dimensions]
notes: interaction patterns, oscillation, destabilization
```

---

# **6. Resilience Analyzer**  
### **Purpose**  
Assess system resilience to conditions or stacks.

### **Inputs**
```
Condition_Vector
Resilience_Profile
```

### **Outputs**
```
Resilience_Score
Failure_Modes
```

### **Resilience Equation**

\[
R(d) = \frac{Resilience(d)}{C(d)}
\]

Aggregate resilience:

\[
R_{total} = \sum_{d} R(d)
\]

### **Interpretation**
- **High R(d):** dimension absorbs condition  
- **Low R(d):** dimension vulnerable  
- **Low R_total:** system at risk  

### **Failure Modes**
```
informational_collapse
temporal_overload
structural_lockout
emotional_break
governance_instability
economic_shock
computational_failure
social_fragmentation
```

---

# **7. Analyzer Integration Map**

Analyzers integrate with:

- **Condition Capture**  
- **Dimensional Model**  
- **Condition Stacks**  
- **Regime Maps**  
- **Hooks & Stacks Substrate**  
- **Incident Substrate**  
- **Resilience Checker**  
- **Governance / Media / Structural Substrates**

Analyzers are the **computational backbone** of the Conditions Substrate Model.

---

# **8. Operator Notes**
- Always compute **C(d)** before regime analysis.  
- Always compute **C_total** before stack analysis.  
- Always compute **interaction terms** for stacks.  
- Always compute **resilience** for high‑impact fields.  
- Oscillation requires special attention.  
- Critical thresholds must be monitored.  

---

# `c_Propagation.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Cross‑Module Propagation**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Cross‑Module Propagation Engine  
**Purpose:** Define how conditions move, transform, and influence other modules across the TriadicFrameworks canon.

Conditions are **ambient fields**.  
Propagation describes **how those fields travel through the system**.

---

# **2. Propagation Overview**

Propagation is the process by which a condition:

1. **enters** the system  
2. **couples** to dimensions  
3. **interacts** with other modules  
4. **modifies** regime states  
5. **produces** downstream effects  

Propagation is **not linear**.  
It is **field‑based**, **dimensional**, and **module‑dependent**.

---

# **3. Propagation Equation (Core Engine)**

Propagation across modules is governed by:

\[
P_{module}(d) = C(d) \cdot G_{module}(d)
\]

Where:

- **\( C(d) \)** — condition field in dimension \( d \)  
- **\( G_{module}(d) \)** — module‑specific gain coefficient  
- **\( P_{module}(d) \)** — propagated field strength inside the module  

Aggregate propagation:

\[
P_{total} = \sum_{d=1}^{n} P_{module}(d)
\]

This determines how strongly a condition influences a module.

---

# **4. Propagation Pathways (Canonical)**

Conditions propagate through **five canonical pathways**:

### **4.1 Structural Pathway**
Flows through:

- Structural Detection  
- Opacity  
- Mode  
- TEL  

Structural conditions modify:

- access  
- pathways  
- bottlenecks  
- physical constraints  

Propagation tends to **narrow** or **stabilize** regimes.

---

### **4.2 Informational Pathway**
Flows through:

- Media Substrate Model  
- Narrative Engine  
- Attention Systems  

Informational conditions modify:

- clarity  
- noise  
- overload  
- narrative susceptibility  

Propagation tends to **narrow** or **oscillate** regimes.

---

### **4.3 Emotional Pathway**
Flows through:

- Emotions Substrate  
- Identity Resonance  
- Social Substrate  

Emotional conditions modify:

- affective baseline  
- resonance  
- vulnerability  

Propagation tends to **expand** or **narrow** regimes.

---

### **4.4 Temporal Pathway**
Flows through:

- Temporal Engine  
- Rhythmic Lifting  
- Drift/Coherence Layers  

Temporal conditions modify:

- pacing  
- compression  
- rhythm  

Propagation tends to **narrow**, **expand**, or **oscillate** regimes.

---

### **4.5 Governance Pathway**
Flows through:

- Governance Substrate  
- Legitimacy Engine  
- Enforcement Envelope  

Governance conditions modify:

- rule clarity  
- opacity  
- stability  

Propagation tends to **stabilize** or **oscillate** regimes.

---

# **5. Module‑Specific Propagation Maps**

Below are the canonical propagation maps for each major module.

---

## **5.1 Structural Detection Module**
**Receives:** structural carriers, scarcity, bottlenecks  
**Outputs:** structural signatures, access maps  
**Propagation:** high in D0, moderate in D7  
**Effect:** narrowing or stabilizing

---

## **5.2 Emotions Substrate**
**Receives:** emotional carriers, isolation, belonging  
**Outputs:** affective resonance fields  
**Propagation:** high in D2, moderate in D4  
**Effect:** expanding or narrowing

---

## **5.3 Media Substrate Model**
**Receives:** informational carriers, noise, clarity  
**Outputs:** narrative density, signal/noise ratios  
**Propagation:** extreme in D1  
**Effect:** narrowing or oscillating

---

## **5.4 Governance Substrate Model**
**Receives:** governance carriers, opacity, legitimacy  
**Outputs:** enforcement envelope, rule stability  
**Propagation:** high in D7, moderate in D0  
**Effect:** stabilizing or oscillating

---

## **5.5 Opacity / Mode / TEL**
**Receives:** structural + informational + governance fields  
**Outputs:** echo‑state, polarity, mode constraints  
**Propagation:** multi‑dimensional  
**Effect:** narrowing, stabilizing, oscillating

---

## **5.6 Incident Substrate Model**
**Receives:** acute condition spikes  
**Outputs:** incident signatures, shock profiles  
**Propagation:** high across all dimensions  
**Effect:** destabilizing

---

## **5.7 Resilience Checker**
**Receives:** C(d), C_total  
**Outputs:** resilience score, failure modes  
**Propagation:** inverse of condition strength  
**Effect:** stabilizing or failure

---

# **6. Propagation Stages**

Propagation occurs in **four canonical stages**:

### **Stage 1 — Ingress**
Condition enters the system through its carrier.

### **Stage 2 — Dimensional Coupling**
Condition attaches to dimensions via \( M(d) \) and \( F(d) \).

### **Stage 3 — Module Gain**
Modules amplify or dampen the condition via \( G_{module}(d) \).

### **Stage 4 — Regime Modification**
Condition alters regime state:

- narrowed  
- expanded  
- oscillating  
- stabilized  
- destabilized  

---

# **7. Propagation Examples**

### **Example 1 — Informational Noise → Media → Attention**
```
Carrier: informational
Modifier: noise
Target: attention
Effect: narrowing
```

Propagation:
- High D1 → Media Substrate  
- Media amplifies → Attention collapse  
- Regime → narrowed

---

### **Example 2 — Temporal Compression → Rhythmic Lifting**
```
Carrier: temporal
Modifier: compression
Target: decision_bandwidth
Effect: narrowing
```

Propagation:
- High D3 → Temporal Engine  
- Compression → drift acceleration  
- Regime → severe narrowing

---

### **Example 3 — Governance Opacity → Narrative Oscillation**
```
Carrier: governance
Modifier: opacity
Target: narrative_susceptibility
Effect: oscillating
```

Propagation:
- High D7 → Governance Substrate  
- Opacity → informational instability  
- Regime → oscillating

---

# **8. Operator Notes**

- Propagation is **dimensional first**, **module second**.  
- High \( C(d) \) in D1, D3, D7 → oscillation risk.  
- High \( C(d) \) in D0, D4, D7 → stabilization potential.  
- Incident Substrate amplifies all fields.  
- Resilience Checker must run after propagation.  

---

# `c_Regime.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Regime Transitions & Thresholds**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Regime Transition Engine  
**Purpose:** Define how conditions and condition stacks produce regime transitions, including thresholds, dimensional signatures, oscillation patterns, and destabilization modes.

Regimes are **dynamic operating states** of a system.  
Conditions modify regimes by altering dimensional fields.

---

# **2. Canonical Regime States**

The Conditions Substrate Model recognizes **six** canonical regime states:

```
baseline
narrowed
expanded
oscillating
stabilized
destabilized
```

Each regime is defined by:

- dimensional field patterns  
- aggregate condition magnitude  
- dominant Effects  
- interaction signatures  

---

# **3. Regime Transition Equation**

Regime transitions are determined by:

\[
Regime_{new} = f(C(d),\; C_{total},\; Effect,\; Thresholds(d),\; Interactions)
\]

Where:

- **C(d)** — dimensional field  
- **C_total** — aggregate condition magnitude  
- **Effect** — narrowing, expanding, oscillating, stabilizing, destabilizing  
- **Thresholds(d)** — dimension‑specific critical points  
- **Interactions** — reinforcing, counteracting, oscillatory, critical‑point amplification  

---

# **4. Regime Thresholds (Critical Points)**

Each dimension has thresholds that determine regime transitions.

### **Threshold Types**
```
narrow_threshold(d)
expand_threshold(d)
oscillate_threshold(d)
stabilize_threshold(d)
destabilize_threshold(d)
```

### **Threshold Logic**
- If **C(d) > narrow_threshold(d)** → narrowing  
- If **C(d) > expand_threshold(d)** → expanding  
- If **C(d) > oscillate_threshold(d)** → oscillating  
- If **C(d) > destabilize_threshold(d)** → destabilized  
- If **C(d) > stabilize_threshold(d)** → stabilized  

Thresholds vary by dimension and system type.

---

# **5. Regime Signatures (Dimensional Patterns)**

Each regime has a distinct dimensional signature.

---

## **5.1 Narrowed Regime**
**Signature:**
- high C(d) in **D1 informational**, **D3 temporal**, **D0 structural**  
- dominant Effects: **narrowing**  
- reduced decision bandwidth  
- compressed attention  

**Common Causes:**
- informational noise  
- temporal compression  
- structural scarcity  

---

## **5.2 Expanded Regime**
**Signature:**
- high C(d) in **D2 emotional**, **D4 social**  
- dominant Effects: **expanding**  
- increased creativity, exploration, identity resonance  

**Common Causes:**
- emotional belonging  
- social cohesion  
- informational clarity  

---

## **5.3 Oscillating Regime**
**Signature:**
- alternating high/low C(d) in **D1**, **D3**, **D7**  
- dominant Effects: **oscillating**  
- unstable narrative processing  
- alternating trust/doubt cycles  

**Common Causes:**
- governance opacity  
- informational overload  
- temporal instability  

---

## **5.4 Stabilized Regime**
**Signature:**
- high C(d) in **D4**, **D7**  
- dominant Effects: **stabilizing**  
- reduced drift  
- increased coherence  

**Common Causes:**
- social cohesion  
- governance legitimacy  
- informational clarity  

---

## **5.5 Destabilized Regime**
**Signature:**
- high C(d) in **D5 economic**, **D7 governance**, **D1 informational**  
- dominant Effects: **destabilizing**  
- breakdown of coherence  
- volatility spikes  

**Common Causes:**
- economic volatility  
- governance instability  
- informational shock  

---

# **6. Regime Transition Map**

Below is the canonical transition map.

```
baseline → narrowed
baseline → expanded
baseline → oscillating
baseline → stabilized
baseline → destabilized

narrowed → destabilized
expanded → destabilized
oscillating → failure
stabilized → narrowed (under compression)
stabilized → expanded (under resonance)
```

Transitions depend on:

- dimensional spikes  
- aggregate magnitude  
- interaction patterns  
- critical thresholds  

---

# **7. Regime Transition Examples**

### **Example 1 — Informational Noise → Narrowed**
```
C(D1) = very high
Effect = narrowing
Regime = narrowed
```

### **Example 2 — Emotional Belonging → Expanded**
```
C(D2) = high
Effect = expanding
Regime = expanded
```

### **Example 3 — Governance Opacity → Oscillating**
```
C(D7) = high
Effect = oscillating
Regime = oscillating
```

### **Example 4 — Economic Volatility → Destabilized**
```
C(D5) = extreme
Effect = destabilizing
Regime = destabilized
```

### **Example 5 — Stabilization Stack → Stabilized**
```
C(D4) = high
C(D7) = high
Effect = stabilizing
Regime = stabilized
```

---

# **8. Regime Failure Modes**

Oscillation or destabilization can escalate into failure:

```
informational collapse
temporal overload
structural lockout
emotional break
social fragmentation
economic shock
governance instability
computational failure
```

Failure modes are detected by:

- extreme C(d)  
- multi‑dimensional spikes  
- oscillation persistence  
- threshold cascades  

---

# **9. Integration Map**

Regime transitions integrate with:

- Condition Capture  
- Dimensional Model  
- Condition Stacks  
- Analyzer Suite  
- Structural Detection  
- Media Substrate  
- Governance Substrate  
- Incident Substrate  
- Resilience Checker  

Regimes are the **dynamic output** of the Conditions Substrate Model.

# `c_Hooks.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Hooks & Attachment Points**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Attachment & Influence Engine  
**Purpose:** Define the canonical hooks through which conditions attach to a system, exert dimensional influence, and propagate into regime transitions.

Hooks are **attachment points**.  
They determine *where* and *how* a condition enters the system.

---

# **2. What is a Hook?**

A **Hook** is a structural or cognitive attachment point that allows a condition to influence a system.

Formally:

\[
Hook = (Attachment\_Point,\; Dimension,\; Sensitivity)
\]

Where:

- **Attachment_Point** — the subsystem the condition binds to  
- **Dimension** — the RTT dimension through which influence propagates  
- **Sensitivity** — how strongly the system responds  

Hooks are **not** conditions.  
Hooks are **interfaces** that conditions use.

---

# **3. Canonical Hook Set**

Below is the canonical hook set for the Conditions Substrate Model.

```
cognitive_load
emotional_vulnerability
structural_bottleneck
narrative_anchor
identity_resonance
resource_gate
time_gate
social_cohesion_point
computational_pressure_point
governance_opacity_point
```

Each hook corresponds to a **dimension** and a **regime target**.

---

# **4. Hook Definitions**

### **4.1 Cognitive Load**
**Dimension:** D1 informational  
**Attachment:** attention, decision_bandwidth  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** noise, overload, compression  
**Effect:** narrowing  

---

### **4.2 Emotional Vulnerability**
**Dimension:** D2 emotional  
**Attachment:** emotional_baseline  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** isolation, fear, instability  
**Effect:** narrowing or expanding  

---

### **4.3 Structural Bottleneck**
**Dimension:** D0 structural  
**Attachment:** mobility, resource_access  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** scarcity, access constraints  
**Effect:** narrowing  

---

### **4.4 Narrative Anchor**
**Dimension:** D1 informational  
**Attachment:** narrative_susceptibility  
**Sensitivity:** medium–high  
**Activated by:** clarity, framing, repetition  
**Effect:** stabilizing or narrowing  

---

### **4.5 Identity Resonance**
**Dimension:** D2 emotional / D4 social  
**Attachment:** identity_baseline  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** belonging, cohesion, threat  
**Effect:** expanding or destabilizing  

---

### **4.6 Resource Gate**
**Dimension:** D0 structural / D5 economic  
**Attachment:** resource_access  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** scarcity, incentives  
**Effect:** narrowing or oscillating  

---

### **4.7 Time Gate**
**Dimension:** D3 temporal  
**Attachment:** time_window  
**Sensitivity:** extreme  
**Activated by:** compression, acceleration  
**Effect:** narrowing  

---

### **4.8 Social Cohesion Point**
**Dimension:** D4 social  
**Attachment:** group identity  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** cohesion, norms  
**Effect:** stabilizing  

---

### **4.9 Computational Pressure Point**
**Dimension:** D6 computational  
**Attachment:** throughput, latency  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** overload, optimization failure  
**Effect:** narrowing  

---

### **4.10 Governance Opacity Point**
**Dimension:** D7 governance  
**Attachment:** rule clarity, legitimacy  
**Sensitivity:** high  
**Activated by:** opacity, instability  
**Effect:** oscillating or destabilizing  

---

# **5. Hook Activation Logic**

Hooks activate when:

\[
M(d) \cdot F(d) > Hook\_Threshold(d)
\]

Where:

- **M(d)** = modifier intensity  
- **F(d)** = coupling coefficient  
- **Hook_Threshold(d)** = minimum field strength required  

Activation produces:

- dimensional spikes  
- regime shifts  
- propagation into other modules  

---

# **6. Hooks in Condition Stacks**

Hooks determine **how conditions interact** inside stacks.

### **6.1 Reinforcing Hooks**
Multiple conditions attach to the **same hook**:

- cognitive_load + informational_noise  
- structural_bottleneck + resource_scarcity  

Produces **super‑linear narrowing**.

---

### **6.2 Counteracting Hooks**
Conditions attach to **opposing hooks**:

- emotional_belonging vs emotional_isolation  
- narrative_clarity vs narrative_noise  

Produces **partial cancellation**.

---

### **6.3 Oscillation Hooks**
Conditions attach to hooks that naturally oscillate:

- governance_opacity_point  
- temporal_instability  
- informational_overload  

Produces **oscillating regimes**.

---

### **6.4 Critical Hooks**
Hooks with extremely low thresholds:

- time_gate  
- governance_opacity_point  
- computational_pressure_point  

Small conditions → **large regime shifts**.

---

# **7. Hook–Dimension Map**

```
D0 Structural → structural_bottleneck, resource_gate
D1 Informational → cognitive_load, narrative_anchor
D2 Emotional → emotional_vulnerability, identity_resonance
D3 Temporal → time_gate
D4 Social → social_cohesion_point
D5 Economic → resource_gate
D6 Computational → computational_pressure_point
D7 Governance → governance_opacity_point
```

Hooks are the **interfaces** between conditions and dimensions.

---

# **8. Hook Examples**

### **Example 1 — Informational Noise**
```
Condition: informational noise → narrowing
Hook: cognitive_load
Dimension: D1
Effect: narrowed regime
```

### **Example 2 — Temporal Compression**
```
Condition: temporal compression → narrowing
Hook: time_gate
Dimension: D3
Effect: severe narrowing
```

### **Example 3 — Governance Opacity**
```
Condition: governance opacity → oscillating
Hook: governance_opacity_point
Dimension: D7
Effect: oscillating regime
```

---

# **9. Integration Map**

Hooks integrate with:

- Condition Capture  
- Dimensional Model  
- Condition Stacks  
- Regime Maps  
- Structural Detection  
- Media Substrate  
- Governance Substrate  
- Incident Substrate

Hooks are the **mechanism layer** of the Conditions Substrate Model.

# `c_Thresholds.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Dimension‑Specific Critical Points**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Critical Threshold Engine  
**Purpose:** Define the critical points at which dimensional fields trigger regime transitions, oscillation, destabilization, or failure.

Thresholds determine **when** a condition becomes powerful enough to alter the system’s regime.

---

# **2. Threshold Equation (Core Engine)**

Each dimension has a critical threshold:

\[
Threshold(d) = f(Sensitivity(d),\; Baseline(d),\; Resilience(d))
\]

A condition triggers a regime transition when:

\[
C(d) > Threshold(d)
\]

Where:

- **C(d)** — condition field in dimension \( d \)  
- **Threshold(d)** — critical point for that dimension  
- **Sensitivity(d)** — system responsiveness  
- **Baseline(d)** — current regime state  
- **Resilience(d)** — resistance to change  

---

# **3. Threshold Types**

Each dimension has five threshold categories:

```
narrow_threshold(d)
expand_threshold(d)
oscillate_threshold(d)
stabilize_threshold(d)
destabilize_threshold(d)
```

Thresholds are **dimension‑specific** and **nonlinear**.

---

# **4. Canonical Threshold Map (D0–D7)**

Below is the full RTT threshold specification.

---

## **D0 — Structural Thresholds**
**Domain:** access, pathways, constraints  
**High sensitivity:** bottlenecks, scarcity  
**Thresholds:**
```
narrow_threshold(D0) = medium
stabilize_threshold(D0) = high
destabilize_threshold(D0) = extreme
```

**Notes:**  
Structural compression → narrowing.  
Structural instability → destabilization.

---

## **D1 — Informational Thresholds**
**Domain:** clarity, noise, overload  
**High sensitivity:** cognitive load  
**Thresholds:**
```
narrow_threshold(D1) = low
oscillate_threshold(D1) = medium
destabilize_threshold(D1) = high
```

**Notes:**  
Informational noise triggers narrowing early.  
Overload + opacity → oscillation.

---

## **D2 — Emotional Thresholds**
**Domain:** affective baseline, resonance  
**High sensitivity:** vulnerability  
**Thresholds:**
```
expand_threshold(D2) = low
narrow_threshold(D2) = medium
destabilize_threshold(D2) = high
```

**Notes:**  
Belonging expands early.  
Fear narrows.  
Instability destabilizes.

---

## **D3 — Temporal Thresholds**
**Domain:** pacing, compression, rhythm  
**High sensitivity:** time_gate  
**Thresholds:**
```
narrow_threshold(D3) = very low
oscillate_threshold(D3) = medium
destabilize_threshold(D3) = high
```

**Notes:**  
Temporal compression is extremely potent.  
Temporal instability → oscillation.

---

## **D4 — Social Thresholds**
**Domain:** cohesion, exclusion, norms  
**High sensitivity:** identity resonance  
**Thresholds:**
```
stabilize_threshold(D4) = low
expand_threshold(D4) = medium
destabilize_threshold(D4) = high
```

**Notes:**  
Cohesion stabilizes early.  
Fragmentation destabilizes.

---

## **D5 — Economic Thresholds**
**Domain:** scarcity, incentives, volatility  
**High sensitivity:** resource_gate  
**Thresholds:**
```
narrow_threshold(D5) = medium
oscillate_threshold(D5) = medium-high
destabilize_threshold(D5) = high
```

**Notes:**  
Volatility → oscillation.  
Extreme scarcity → destabilization.

---

## **D6 — Computational Thresholds**
**Domain:** latency, load, throughput  
**High sensitivity:** pressure_point  
**Thresholds:**
```
narrow_threshold(D6) = low
stabilize_threshold(D6) = medium
destabilize_threshold(D6) = high
```

**Notes:**  
Overload narrows.  
Optimization stabilizes.

---

## **D7 — Governance Thresholds**
**Domain:** rules, legitimacy, opacity  
**High sensitivity:** opacity_point  
**Thresholds:**
```
oscillate_threshold(D7) = low
stabilize_threshold(D7) = medium
destabilize_threshold(D7) = high
```

**Notes:**  
Opacity → oscillation early.  
Instability → destabilization.

---

# **5. Threshold Activation Logic**

A threshold is crossed when:

\[
M(d) \cdot F(d) > Threshold(d)
\]

Where:

- **M(d)** — modifier intensity  
- **F(d)** — coupling coefficient  

Activation produces:

- dimensional spikes  
- regime transitions  
- stack amplification  
- propagation into other modules  

---

# **6. Threshold Cascades**

Thresholds can cascade across dimensions:

### **6.1 Narrowing Cascade**
D1 → D3 → D0  
(noise → compression → bottleneck)

### **6.2 Oscillation Cascade**
D7 → D1 → D3  
(opacity → overload → instability)

### **6.3 Destabilization Cascade**
D5 → D7 → D1  
(volatility → governance instability → informational collapse)

Cascades are detected by:

\[
C(d_i) > Threshold(d_i) \quad \text{for multiple } d_i
\]

---

# **7. Threshold Examples**

### **Example 1 — Informational Noise**
```
C(D1) = high
Threshold(D1) = low
→ narrowed
```

### **Example 2 — Temporal Compression**
```
C(D3) = extreme
Threshold(D3) = very low
→ severe narrowing
```

### **Example 3 — Governance Opacity**
```
C(D7) = medium
Threshold(D7) = low
→ oscillating
```

### **Example 4 — Economic Volatility**
```
C(D5) = high
Threshold(D5) = high
→ destabilized
```

---

Thresholds integrate with:

- Condition Capture  
- Dimensional Model  
- Condition Stacks  
- Analyzer Suite  
- Regime Maps  
- Hooks & Attachment Points  
- Propagation Engine  
- Incident Substrate  
- Resilience Checker  

Thresholds are the **critical decision layer** of the Conditions Substrate Model.

# `c_Signatures.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Regime Signature Library**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Regime Signature Engine  
**Purpose:** Provide canonical signatures for all regime states, enabling operators and analyzers to identify regime patterns from dimensional fields, condition stacks, and propagation behavior.

A **Regime Signature** is a recognizable pattern of dimensional activation, effects, and interactions.

---

# **2. Signature Structure**

A regime signature is defined as:

\[
Signature = (Dimensional\_Pattern,\; Effect\_Profile,\; Interaction\_Profile,\; Threshold\_Crossings)
\]

Where:

- **Dimensional_Pattern** — which dimensions spike  
- **Effect_Profile** — dominant Effects (narrowing, expanding, oscillating, etc.)  
- **Interaction_Profile** — reinforcing, counteracting, oscillatory, critical  
- **Threshold_Crossings** — which thresholds were exceeded  

---

# **3. Canonical Regime Signatures**

Below is the full signature library for all six canonical regimes.

---

## **3.1 Baseline Regime Signature**
```
Dimensional Pattern: low–medium across all dimensions
Effect Profile: neutral
Interaction Profile: none
Threshold Crossings: none
```

**Notes:**  
System is stable, coherent, and uncompressed.  
Baseline is the reference state for all transitions.

---

## **3.2 Narrowed Regime Signature**
```
Dimensional Pattern:
  - D1 informational: high
  - D3 temporal: high
  - D0 structural: medium–high

Effect Profile:
  - dominant narrowing
  - compression of bandwidth

Interaction Profile:
  - reinforcing (noise + compression + scarcity)

Threshold Crossings:
  - narrow_threshold(D1)
  - narrow_threshold(D3)
```

**Behavioral Indicators:**  
- reduced decision bandwidth  
- tunnel vision  
- increased error rate  

---

## **3.3 Expanded Regime Signature**
```
Dimensional Pattern:
  - D2 emotional: high
  - D4 social: high

Effect Profile:
  - dominant expanding
  - increased exploratory behavior

Interaction Profile:
  - reinforcing (belonging + cohesion)

Threshold Crossings:
  - expand_threshold(D2)
  - expand_threshold(D4)
```

**Behavioral Indicators:**  
- creativity  
- openness  
- increased identity resonance  

---

## **3.4 Oscillating Regime Signature**
```
Dimensional Pattern:
  - D1 informational: alternating high/low
  - D3 temporal: alternating high/low
  - D7 governance: high

Effect Profile:
  - dominant oscillating
  - alternating narrowing/expanding

Interaction Profile:
  - oscillatory (opacity + overload + instability)

Threshold Crossings:
  - oscillate_threshold(D7)
  - oscillate_threshold(D1)
```

**Behavioral Indicators:**  
- unstable narratives  
- alternating trust/doubt  
- inconsistent decision patterns  

---

## **3.5 Stabilized Regime Signature**
```
Dimensional Pattern:
  - D4 social: high
  - D7 governance: medium–high

Effect Profile:
  - dominant stabilizing
  - reduced drift

Interaction Profile:
  - reinforcing (cohesion + legitimacy)

Threshold Crossings:
  - stabilize_threshold(D4)
  - stabilize_threshold(D7)
```

**Behavioral Indicators:**  
- coherence  
- predictability  
- reduced volatility  

---

## **3.6 Destabilized Regime Signature**
```
Dimensional Pattern:
  - D5 economic: high
  - D7 governance: high
  - D1 informational: high

Effect Profile:
  - dominant destabilizing
  - breakdown of coherence

Interaction Profile:
  - critical-point amplification (volatility + opacity + overload)

Threshold Crossings:
  - destabilize_threshold(D5)
  - destabilize_threshold(D7)
```

**Behavioral Indicators:**  
- volatility  
- fragmentation  
- collapse of stable patterns  

---

# **4. Signature Detection Logic**

Signatures are detected using:

\[
Signature(d) = Pattern(d) + Effect(d) + Interaction(d) + Threshold(d)
\]

A regime is identified when:

- dimensional patterns match  
- dominant Effects align  
- interaction profiles confirm  
- thresholds are crossed  

Detection is performed by the **Regime Shift Analyzer**.

---

# **5. Signature Examples**

### **Example — Narrowed Signature**
```
D1 = very high
D3 = high
Effect = narrowing
Interaction = reinforcing
→ narrowed regime
```

### **Example — Oscillating Signature**
```

D1 = alternating

Interaction = oscillatory
→ oscillating regime
```

### **Example — Stabilized Signature**
```

→ stabilized regime
```

---

# **6. Signature Cascades**

Signatures can cascade:

### **6.1 Narrowing → Destabilized**
D1 spike → D3 spike → D5 spike  
(noise → compression → volatility)

### **6.2 Stabilized → Narrowed**
D4 stabilizing → D3 compression  
(cohesion → time pressure)

### **6.3 Oscillating → Failure**
Persistent oscillation → threshold cascade  
(opacity → overload → instability)

# `c_Flows.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Condition Flow Diagrams**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Flow & Pathway Engine  
**Purpose:** Provide canonical flow diagrams showing how conditions move through the system, attach to hooks, couple to dimensions, propagate across modules, and produce regime transitions.

Flows describe **how conditions travel**.  
Stacks describe **how conditions combine**.  
Dimensions describe **how conditions act**.

---

# **2. Flow Architecture Overview**

A condition flows through the system in **five canonical stages**:

```
Ingress → Hook Attachment → Dimensional Coupling → Module Propagation → Regime Transition
```

This is the universal flow for all conditions.

---

# **3. Master Flow Diagram (Text‑Form)**

```
[Condition]
   ↓
(Carrier)
   ↓
[Ingress]
   ↓
(Hook Attachment)
   ↓
[Dimensional Coupling]
   ↓
(Module Propagation)
   ↓
[Regime Transition]
   ↓
(System State Update)
```

This is the backbone of the Conditions Substrate Model.

---

# **4. Stage 1 — Ingress Flow**

Ingress is how a condition enters the system.

### **Ingress Sources**
```
environmental input
informational input
emotional input
temporal input
social input
economic input
computational input
governance input
```

### **Ingress Diagram**
```
Carrier → Ingress → Hook
```

Ingress determines **which hooks activate first**.

---

# **5. Stage 2 — Hook Attachment Flow**

Hooks are attachment points that determine where the condition binds.

### **Hook Flow Diagram**
```
Condition → Hook → Dimension
```

### **Hook Activation Logic**
\[
M(d) \cdot F(d) > Hook\_Threshold(d)
\]

### **Hook Examples**
```
informational noise → cognitive_load
temporal compression → time_gate
governance opacity → governance_opacity_point
```

Hooks determine **initial dimensional impact**.

---

# **6. Stage 3 — Dimensional Coupling Flow**

Dimensional coupling determines how strongly the condition affects each dimension.

### **Dimensional Flow Diagram**
```
Hook → Dimension → C(d)
```

### **Dimensional Equation**
\[
C(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

### **Dimensional Flow Notes**
- D1, D3, D7 → oscillation risk  
- D0, D4, D7 → stabilization potential  
- D2, D4 → expansion potential  

Dimensions determine **regime direction**.

---

# **7. Stage 4 — Module Propagation Flow**

Propagation determines how conditions influence other modules.

### **Propagation Flow Diagram**
```
Dimension → Module Gain → Propagated Field
```

### **Propagation Equation**
\[
P_{module}(d) = C(d) \cdot G_{module}(d)
\]

### **Propagation Targets**
```
Structural Detection
Emotions Substrate
Media Substrate
Governance Substrate
Temporal Engine
Incident Substrate
Resilience Checker
```

Propagation determines **system‑wide effects**.

---

# **8. Stage 5 — Regime Transition Flow**

Regime transitions occur when dimensional fields exceed thresholds.

### **Regime Flow Diagram**
```
Propagated Field → Thresholds → Regime State
```

### **Threshold Logic**
\[
C(d) > Threshold(d)
\]

### **Regime States**
```
baseline
narrowed
expanded
oscillating
stabilized
destabilized
```

Regime transitions determine **system behavior**.

---

# **9. Full Condition Flow (End‑to‑End)**

```
Condition
  ↓
Carrier
  ↓
Ingress
  ↓
Hook Attachment
  ↓
Dimensional Coupling
  ↓
Module Propagation
  ↓
Threshold Crossings
  ↓
Regime Transition
  ↓
System State Update
```

This is the **complete flow** for any condition.

---

# **10. Flow Examples**

### **Example 1 — Informational Noise**
```
Carrier: informational
Ingress → cognitive_load
Hook → D1
C(D1) = high
Propagation → Media Substrate
Threshold → narrow_threshold(D1)
Regime → narrowed
```

### **Example 2 — Temporal Compression**
```

Ingress → time_gate

```

### **Example 3 — Governance Opacity**
```

Ingress → governance_opacity_point
Hook → D7
C(D7) = high
Propagation → Governance Substrate
Threshold → oscillate_threshold(D7)
Regime → oscillating
```

---

# **11. Flow Cascades**

Flows can cascade across dimensions:

### **Narrowing Cascade**
```
D1 → D3 → D0
(noise → compression → bottleneck)
```

### **Oscillation Cascade**
```
D7 → D1 → D3
(opacity → overload → instability)
```

### **Destabilization Cascade**
```
D5 → D7 → D1
(volatility → governance instability → informational collapse)
```

Cascades produce **multi‑dimensional regime shifts**.

---

# **12. Integration Map**

Flows integrate with:

- Condition Capture  
- Dimensional Model  
- Condition Stacks  
- Analyzer Suite

- Threshold Engine

# `c_Drift.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Drift & Coherence Integration**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Drift/Coherence Integration Layer  
**Purpose:** Define how conditions generate drift, suppress coherence, amplify instability, or reinforce stability across RTT dimensions and modules.

Conditions do not only shift regimes —  
they also **push systems off their baseline trajectory**.

This file explains **how**.

---

# **2. Drift & Coherence Fundamentals**

### **Drift**  
A **directional deviation** from baseline behavior caused by persistent dimensional pressure.

\[
Drift(d) = C(d) \cdot DriftGain(d)
\]

### **Coherence**  
A **stability envelope** that resists drift.

\[
Coherence(d) = \frac{CoherenceBase(d)}{C(d)}
\]

### **Drift–Coherence Interaction**
\[
Drift(d) > Coherence(d) \Rightarrow \text{Coherence Break}
\]

---

# **3. Drift Sources (Condition‑Driven)**

Conditions generate drift through:

### **3.1 Dimensional Pressure**
High C(d) → directional push  
(e.g., informational noise → attention drift)

### **3.2 Hook Activation**
Hooks amplify drift:
- cognitive_load  
- time_gate  
- governance_opacity_point  
- identity_resonance  

### **3.3 Stack Reinforcement**
Multiple conditions → super‑linear drift

### **3.4 Threshold Cascades**
Crossing narrow/oscillate/destabilize thresholds → drift acceleration

---

# **4. Coherence Sources (Condition‑Driven)**

Conditions can also **increase coherence**:

### **4.1 Stabilizing Effects**
- social cohesion  
- governance legitimacy  
- informational clarity  

### **4.2 Dimensional Balance**
Balanced C(d) across D2, D4, D7 → coherence reinforcement

### **4.3 Stack Stabilization**
Stabilizing stacks → coherence envelope

### **4.4 Drift Dampening**
High coherence(d) → drift suppression

---

# **5. Drift Equation (Full Form)**

\[
Drift(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d) \cdot DriftGain(d) \cdot HookGain(d)
\]

Where:

- **M(d)** — modifier intensity  
- **F(d)** — coupling coefficient  
- **DriftGain(d)** — dimension’s drift susceptibility  
- **HookGain(d)** — hook amplification factor  

### **DriftGain(d) Canonical Values**
```
D1 informational = high
D3 temporal = high
D7 governance = medium-high
D2 emotional = medium
D4 social = medium
D0 structural = low-medium
D5 economic = medium-high
D6 computational = medium
```

---

# **6. Coherence Equation (Full Form)**

\[
Coherence(d) = \frac{CoherenceBase(d) + StabilizingField(d)}{C(d)}
\]

Where:

- **CoherenceBase(d)** — baseline stability  
- **StabilizingField(d)** — stabilizing conditions (clarity, cohesion, legitimacy)  

### **CoherenceBase(d) Canonical Values**
```
D4 social = high
D7 governance = high
D1 informational = medium
D2 emotional = medium
D0 structural = medium
D3 temporal = low
D5 economic = low
D6 computational = medium
```

---

# **7. Drift–Coherence Interaction Rules**

### **Rule 1 — Drift Dominance**
If Drift(d) > Coherence(d):
```
→ coherence break
→ regime destabilization
```

### **Rule 2 — Coherence Dominance**
If Coherence(d) > Drift(d):
```
→ drift suppression
→ regime stabilization
```

### **Rule 3 — Oscillation**
If Drift(d) alternates sign or magnitude:
```
→ oscillating regime
```

### **Rule 4 — Critical Drift**
If Drift(d) exceeds destabilize_threshold(d):
```
→ failure mode activation
```

---

# **8. Drift Signatures**

### **Informational Drift**
```
D1 spike + cognitive_load → attention drift
```

### **Temporal Drift**
```
D3 spike + time_gate → pacing drift
```

### **Governance Drift**
```
D7 spike + opacity → legitimacy drift
```

### **Social Drift**
```
D4 fragmentation → identity drift
```

### **Economic Drift**
```
D5 volatility → incentive drift
```

---

# **9. Coherence Signatures**

### **Social Coherence**
```
D4 cohesion → identity stabilization
```

### **Governance Coherence**
```
D7 legitimacy → rule stabilization
```

### **Informational Coherence**
```
D1 clarity → narrative stabilization
```

### **Emotional Coherence**
```
D2 belonging → affective stabilization
```

---

# **10. Drift–Coherence Scenarios**

### **Scenario A — Drift Cascade**
```
C1: informational noise → narrowing
C2: temporal compression → narrowing
C3: governance opacity → oscillating
```

Drift:
```
D1 = extreme
D3 = high
D7 = high
```

Coherence:
```
D4 = low
D7 = low
```

Outcome:
```
Drift > Coherence → destabilized + oscillating
```

---

### **Scenario B — Coherence Envelope**
```
C1: social cohesion → stabilizing
C2: informational clarity → stabilizing
C3: governance legitimacy → stabilizing
```

Drift:
```
D1 = low
D3 = low
D7 = low
```

Coherence:
```
D4 = high
D1 = medium-high
D7 = high
```

Outcome:
```
Coherence > Drift → stabilized + expanded
```

---

# `c_Operators.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Operator Workflows**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Operator Workflow Layer  
**Purpose:** Provide operators with canonical workflows for capturing conditions, computing dimensional fields, evaluating stacks, detecting regime transitions, assessing drift/coherence, and generating actionable outputs.

Operators use this module to **run the system**.

---

# **2. Operator Workflow Overview**

Operators follow a **seven‑step workflow**:

```
1. Capture the condition
2. Assign dimensional intensities
3. Compute C(d) and C_total
4. Identify hooks and attachment points
5. Build or update stacks
6. Evaluate thresholds and regime transitions
7. Assess drift, coherence, and propagation
```

This workflow is universal across all substrates.

---

# **3. Workflow Step 1 — Capture the Condition**

Operators begin with the canonical 4‑tuple:

\[
Condition = (Carrier,\; Modifier,\; Target,\; Effect)
\]

### **Operator Actions**
- Identify the carrier  
- Identify the modifier  
- Identify the target  
- Identify the effect  

### **Checklist**
```
[ ] Carrier identified
[ ] Modifier identified
[ ] Target identified
[ ] Effect identified
```

---

# **4. Workflow Step 2 — Assign Dimensional Intensities**

Operators assign \( M(d) \) values:

\[
M(d) \in \{ none,\; low,\; medium,\; high,\; extreme \}
\]

### **Operator Actions**
- Map condition to relevant dimensions  
- Assign intensities based on domain knowledge  

### **Checklist**
```
[ ] M(d) assigned for all relevant dimensions
[ ] Irrelevant dimensions set to none
```

---

# **5. Workflow Step 3 — Compute Dimensional Fields**

Operators compute:

\[
C(d) = M(d) \cdot F(d)
\]

\[
C_{total} = \sum_{d} C(d)
\]

### **Operator Actions**
- Retrieve coupling coefficients  
- Compute per‑dimension fields  
- Compute aggregate field  

### **Checklist**
```
[ ] C(d) computed
[ ] C_total computed
```

---

# **6. Workflow Step 4 — Identify Hooks**

Hooks determine **where** the condition attaches.

### **Operator Actions**
- Identify activated hooks  
- Evaluate hook thresholds  
- Note amplification factors  

### **Checklist**
```
[ ] Hooks identified
[ ] Hook thresholds checked
[ ] Hook amplification noted
```

---

# **7. Workflow Step 5 — Build or Update Stacks**

Stacks combine multiple conditions:

\[
C_{stack}(d) = \sum w_i(d) \cdot C_i(d) + I(d)
\]

### **Operator Actions**
- Add condition to existing stack  
- Compute weights  
- Compute interaction terms  
- Compute composite fields  

### **Checklist**
```
[ ] Condition added to stack
[ ] w_i(d) assigned
[ ] I(d) computed
[ ] C_stack(d) computed
[ ] C_stack_total computed
```

---

# **8. Workflow Step 6 — Evaluate Thresholds & Regime Transitions**

Operators check:

\[
C(d) > Threshold(d)
\]

### **Operator Actions**
- Compare C(d) to thresholds  
- Identify crossed thresholds  
- Determine regime state  

### **Checklist**
```
[ ] Thresholds checked
[ ] Regime identified
[ ] Transition logged
```

---

# **9. Workflow Step 7 — Assess Drift, Coherence & Propagation**

Operators compute:

\[
Drift(d) = C(d) \cdot DriftGain(d)
\]

\[
Coherence(d) = \frac{CoherenceBase(d)}{C(d)}
\]

### **Operator Actions**
- Compute drift  
- Compute coherence  
- Identify drift‑dominant or coherence‑dominant dimensions  
- Map propagation pathways  

### **Checklist**
```
[ ] Drift computed
[ ] Coherence computed
[ ] Drift vs Coherence evaluated
[ ] Propagation mapped
```

---

# **10. Operator Templates**

### **10.1 Condition Capture Template**
```
Condition:
  Carrier:
  Modifier:
  Target:
  Effect:

Dimensions:
  D0:
  D1:
  D2:
  D3:
  D4:

  D7:

```

```
  - C1: (...)

  - C3: (...)

  w3(d):

Interactions:
  I(d):

Composite:
  C_stack(d):
  C_stack_total:
```

---

### **10.3 Regime Evaluation Template**
```

Regime:
Drivers:
Notes:
```

---

### **10.4 Drift/Coherence Template**
```

Coherence(d):

```

# **3. Example A — Informational Noise Drift**

```

```

\[
Drift(D1) = C(D1) \cdot DriftGain(D1)
\]
```
DriftGain(D1) = high
Drift(D1) = very high × high = extreme
```

### **Coherence Calculation**
\[
Coherence(D1) = \frac{CoherenceBase(D1)}{C(D1)}
\]
```
CoherenceBase(D1) = medium
Coherence(D1) = medium / very high = low
```

### **Drift vs Coherence**
```
Drift(D1) > Coherence(D1)
→ coherence break
```

### **Threshold Crossings**
```
narrow_threshold(D1)
```

```
Regime: narrowed
```

Informational noise produces one of the strongest drift vectors in the substrate.

# `c_Drift_Signatures.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Drift Signature Library**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Drift Signature Engine  
**Purpose:** Provide canonical drift signatures for all dimensions, enabling operators and analyzers to identify drift patterns from condition fields, stacks, hooks, thresholds, and propagation behavior.

A **Drift Signature** is a recognizable pattern of directional deviation caused by persistent dimensional pressure.

---

# **2. Drift Signature Structure**

A drift signature is defined as:

\[
DriftSignature = (DimensionalPressure,\; HookActivation,\; DriftGain,\; CoherenceBreak,\; RegimeShift)
\]

Where:

- **DimensionalPressure** — magnitude and direction of C(d)  
- **HookActivation** — which hooks amplify drift  
- **DriftGain** — dimension’s susceptibility  
- **CoherenceBreak** — whether drift exceeds coherence  
- **RegimeShift** — resulting regime state  

---

# **3. Canonical Drift Signatures (D0–D7)**

Below is the full drift signature library for all RTT dimensions.

---

# **5. Drift Signature Detection Logic**

A drift signature is detected when:

\[
Drift(d) > Coherence(d)
\]

AND

\[
C(d) > DriftThreshold(d)
\]

AND optionally:

\[
HookGain(d) > 0
\]

### **Detection Steps**
1. Compute C(d)  
2. Compute Drift(d)  
3. Compute Coherence(d)  
4. Compare Drift(d) vs Coherence(d)  
5. Check threshold crossings  
6. Identify signature pattern  

---

# `c_Drift_Cascades.md`  
### **Conditions Substrate Model — Multi‑Dimension Drift Cascades**

---

## **1. Identity**  
**Module:** Conditions Substrate Model  
**Role:** Drift Cascade Layer  
**Purpose:** Define multi‑dimension drift cascades — synchronized drift events where multiple dimensions exceed coherence simultaneously, producing large‑scale regime transitions.

Drift cascades are **system‑level events**.  
They represent the highest‑order instability patterns in the substrate.

---

# **2. Cascade Structure**

A drift cascade is defined as:

\[
Cascade = \{ Drift(d_i) > Coherence(d_i) \;\; \forall d_i \in D_{cascade} \}
\]

Where:

- **Drift(d)** — directional deviation  
- **Coherence(d)** — stabilizing envelope  
- **D_cascade** — set of dimensions participating in the cascade  

A cascade occurs when:

1. **Multiple dimensions spike simultaneously**  
2. **Hooks amplify drift across those dimensions**  
3. **Thresholds are crossed in sequence or in parallel**  
4. **Propagation synchronizes drift across modules**

---

# **3. Canonical Drift Cascades (D0–D7)**

Below are the canonical multi‑dimension drift cascades recognized by the Conditions Substrate Model.

---

## **3.1 Informational → Temporal → Structural Cascade**
### **Signature**
```
D1 informational → D3 temporal → D0 structural
```

### **Trigger Conditions**
- informational overload  
- temporal compression  
- structural scarcity  

### **Cascade Behavior**
```
attention drift → pacing drift → access collapse
```

### **Regime**
```
narrowed → severe narrowed → destabilized
```

---

## **3.2 Governance → Informational → Temporal Cascade**
### **Signature**
```
D7 governance → D1 informational → D3 temporal
```

### **Trigger Conditions**
- governance opacity  
- informational noise  
- temporal instability  

### **Cascade Behavior**
```
legitimacy drift → narrative drift → rhythm drift
```

### **Regime**
```
oscillating → oscillating → destabilized
```

---

## **3.3 Economic → Governance → Informational Cascade**
### **Signature**
```
D5 economic → D7 governance → D1 informational
```

### **Trigger Conditions**
- volatility  
- rule instability  
- overload  

### **Cascade Behavior**
```
incentive drift → legitimacy drift → attention drift
```

### **Regime**
```
oscillating → destabilized → collapse
```

---

## **3.4 Emotional → Social → Governance Cascade**
### **Signature**
```
D2 emotional → D4 social → D7 governance
```

### **Trigger Conditions**
- fear  
- fragmentation  
- instability  

### **Cascade Behavior**
```
affective drift → identity drift → legitimacy drift
```

### **Regime**
```
narrowed → destabilized → destabilized
```

---

## **3.5 Structural → Economic → Governance Cascade**
### **Signature**
```
D0 structural → D5 economic → D7 governance
```

### **Trigger Conditions**
- bottleneck  
- scarcity  
- instability  

### **Cascade Behavior**
```
access drift → incentive drift → rule drift
```

### **Regime**
```
narrowed → oscillating → destabilized
```

---

# **4. Cascade Mechanics**

### **4.1 Synchronization**
Cascades synchronize when:

\[
HookGain(d_i) \text{ aligns across dimensions}
\]

Example:
- cognitive_load (D1)  
- time_gate (D3)  
- structural_bottleneck (D0)  

These hooks reinforce each other.

---

### **4.2 Threshold Chains**
Cascades often follow threshold chains:

```
narrow_threshold → oscillate_threshold → destabilize_threshold
```

Example:
- D1 crosses narrow  
- D3 crosses oscillate  
- D7 crosses destabilize  

---

### **4.3 Propagation Loops**
Propagation loops amplify cascades:

```
Media → Temporal → Governance → Media
```

This creates oscillation engines.

---

### **4.4 Coherence Collapse**
Cascades occur when:

\[
Drift(d_i) >> Coherence(d_i)
\]

across multiple dimensions.

---

```

```

---

# **6. Cascade Detection Logic**

A cascade is detected when:

1. **Two or more dimensions exceed coherence**
2. **Thresholds are crossed in sequence or parallel**
3. **Propagation loops reinforce drift**
4. **Hooks amplify drift across dimensions**

### **Detection Formula**
\[
Cascade = \bigcap_{i=1}^{k} \{ Drift(d_i) > Coherence(d_i) \}
\]
